task-0209-count-primes-up-to-n · Count primes up to n

Prompt 
Write `countPrimes(n)` returning the number of prime numbers strictly less than `n`. Use a sieve for efficiency. `n` is in [0, 10^6].

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function countPrimes(n) {
  if (n <= 2) return 0;
  const isComposite = new Uint8Array(n);
  let count = 0;
  for (let i = 2; i < n; i++) {
    if (!isComposite[i]) {
      count++;
      if (i <= Math.sqrt(n)) {
        for (let j = i * i; j < n; j += i) {
          isComposite[j] = 1;
        }
      }
    }
  }
  return count;
}

task-0201-sliding-window-maximum · Sliding window maximum

Prompt 
Return an array of the max of each contiguous window of size k. Example: nums=[1,3,-1,-3,5,3,6,7], k=3 -> [3,3,5,5,6,7].

```python
# BUGGY
def max_sliding_window(nums, k):
    from collections import deque
    q = deque()
    out = []
    for i, n in enumerate(nums):
        while q and nums[q[-1]] <= n:
            q.pop()
        q.append(i)
        if q[0] <= i - k:
            q.popleft()
        if i >= k:
            out.append(nums[q[0]])
    return out
```

task-0208-second-largest-element · Second largest element

Prompt 
Write `second_largest(nums)` in Python returning the second largest DISTINCT element. Return None if there are fewer than two distinct elements.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
def second_largest(nums):
    distinct = set(nums)
    if len(distinct) < 2:
        return None
    distinct.discard(max(distinct))
    return max(distinct)

task-0142-basic-calculator-ii · Basic calculator II

Prompt 
Write `calculate(s)` evaluating a string expression containing non-negative integers and the operators +, -, *, / (integer division truncating). Respect operator precedence. The expression is valid. Example: `calculate("3+2*2")` returns 7.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `calculate`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer 
```javascript
export function calculate(s) {
  const stack = [];
  let num = 0;
  let sign = '+';

  const apply = (op, value) => {
    if (op === '+') stack.push(value);
    else if (op === '-') stack.push(-value);
    else if (op === '*') stack.push(stack.pop() * value);
    else if (op === '/') stack.push(Math.trunc(stack.pop() / value));
  };

  for (let i = 0; i < s.length; i++) {
    const c = s[i];
    if (c >= '0' && c <= '9') {
      num = num * 10 + (c.charCodeAt(0) - 48);
    }
    if ((c < '0' || c > '9') && c !== ' ') {
      apply(sign, num);
      sign = c;
      num = 0;
    }
  }
  apply(sign, num);

  let total = 0;
  for (const v of stack) total += v;
  return total;
}
```
Coding · Bugfix Level 2 PASS 8/8 cases
100%

task-0245-largest-rectangle-in-histogram · Largest rectangle in histogram

Prompt 
Write `largestRectangleArea(heights)` returning the area of the largest rectangle that can be formed within the bar chart `heights` (bars of width 1). Monotonic stack, O(n).

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function largestRectangleArea(heights) {
  const stack = [];
  let maxArea = 0;
  const n = heights.length;
  for (let i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
    const curHeight = i === n ? 0 : heights[i];
    while (stack.length && heights[stack[stack.length - 1]] >= curHeight) {
      const h = heights[stack.pop()];
      const w = stack.length ? i - stack[stack.length - 1] - 1 : i;
      maxArea = Math.max(maxArea, h * w);
    }
    stack.push(i);
  }
  return maxArea;
}

task-0228-subarray-sum-equals-k-count · Subarray sum equals k count

Prompt 
Write `subarraySum(nums, k)` returning the number of contiguous subarrays whose sum equals `k`. Use a prefix-sum hash map for O(n).

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function subarraySum(nums, k) {
  const prefixCounts = new Map();
  prefixCounts.set(0, 1);
  let sum = 0;
  let count = 0;

  for (const num of nums) {
    sum += num;
    const target = sum - k;
    if (prefixCounts.has(target)) {
      count += prefixCounts.get(target);
    }
    prefixCounts.set(sum, (prefixCounts.get(sum) || 0) + 1);
  }

  return count;
}
Code Comprehension Level 3 PASS
100%

task-0029-recursion-depth-output · Recursion depth output

Prompt 
```js
let total = 0;
function count(n) {
  if (n > 2) return;
  total += n;
  count(n + 1);
  total += n;
}
count(0);
console.log(total);
```
What number does this print?
Model answer 
**6**

Tracing the recursion (each call adds `n` before and after recursing):

- `count(0)`: total += 0 → 0, recurse, total += 0
- `count(1)`: total += 1 → 1, recurse, total += 1
- `count(2)`: total += 2 → 3, recurse, total += 2
- `count(3)`: n > 2, returns

Total = 0 + 1 + 2 + 2 + 1 + 0 = **6**

task-0259-count-subsets-summing-to-target · Count subsets summing to target

Prompt 
Write `subset_sum_count(nums, target)` in Python returning the number of subsets (distinct index selections, non-empty or empty is fine) whose sum equals `target`. Values can be non-negative; use DP.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
def subset_sum_count(nums, target):
    dp = [0] * (target + 1)
    dp[0] = 1
    for num in nums:
        for j in range(target, num - 1, -1):
            dp[j] += dp[j - num]
    return dp[target]

task-0305-design-a-self-healing-autoscaling-strate · Design a self-healing autoscaling strategy

Prompt 
Design a resilient autoscaling strategy for an API that must absorb traffic spikes automatically. Describe the ordered design: collect the right metrics (CPU, latency, queue depth), define autoscaling thresholds, run health checks that remove unhealthy instances, automatically scale instance count up and down, and include alerting plus a manual override in case automation misbehaves.
Model answer 
# Resilient Autoscaling Strategy for an API

The system is a **control loop**: measure → decide → act → verify → alert. Below is the ordered design.

---

## 1. Metric Collection

Collect **per-instance** metrics at a fast scrape interval (10–15s), then aggregate to the service level.

| Metric | Why | Aggregation |
|--------|-----|-------------|
| **CPU utilization** | Cheap proxy for compute load | Mean across instances |
| **Request latency** | Direct user-experience signal | p50, **p95, p99** percentiles |
| **Queue depth / in-flight requests** | Predicts imminent saturation before CPU rises | Sum, or per-instance average |
| **Error rate (5xx)** | Detects degradation independent of load | Mean |
| **RPS** | Drives predictive/scheduled scaling | Sum |

**Design notes:**
- Prefer **rate-based** metrics (RPS, in-flight count) for *prediction*, and latency/CPU for *reaction*.
- Use **weighted multi-metric scaling** (e.g., scale on whichever metric breaches first), not CPU alone — latency and queue depth react faster to spikes.
- Store in a time-series DB (Prometheus) and compute short windows (e.g., 1–3 min) to smooth noise.

---

## 2. Autoscaling Thresholds

Use **target-tracking** for steady state and **step/predictive** for spikes.

- **CPU target:** 60–65%. If observed > threshold for *N consecutive periods* (e.g., 2×15s), scale out.
- **Latency target:** p99 < 200ms. Breach → scale out by a step proportional to overshoot.
- **Queue depth target:** > 5 in-flight requests/instance → scale out (this is the earliest spike signal).
- **Cooldowns:** scale-out cooldown short (60–120s); scale-in cooldown long (300–600s) to prevent flapping.
- **Min/max bounds:** e.g., min 2 (high availability), max = capacity budget.
- **Predictive + scheduled scaling:** learn recurring daily/weekly patterns and pre-warm before known peaks.

---

## 3. Health Checks (Remove Unhealthy Instances)

Scaling decisions must be based on **healthy** instance count only.

- **Readiness probe** (`/healthz`): checks process + dependencies (DB, cache, downstream). New instances are *not* sent traffic until ready.
- **Liveness probe**: detects deadlocked/hung processes for restart.
- **On failure:** deregister from the load balancer → stop routing traffic → mark for replacement → the controller increments desired capacity to restore the healthy target.
- **Graceful draining:** on scale-in or failure, drain in-flight requests before termination (terminate-after-drain, not terminate-immediately).

---

## 4. Automatic Scaling Up / Down

**Scale up (aggressive, bounded by max):**
- React immediately when any threshold breaches.
- Add capacity in steps sized to the deficit; consider **burst** capacity for sudden spikes.
- Combine with **rate limiting / circuit breakers** as backpressure if approaching max.

**Scale down (slow, conservative):**
- Only after sustained low load across the long cooldown window.
- Remove the *least-utilized* instances first; always keep ≥ min count.

**Decision function (conceptual):**
```
desired = current_healthy
        + f(CPU, latency, queue_depth)   // reactive
        + predictive_adjustment           // learned patterns
        + scheduled_adjustment            // calendar
clamp(desired, min, max)
```

---

## 5. Alerting + Manual Override

**Alerts (PagerDuty/Slack/email):**
- At **max capacity** for > N minutes → need more headroom or capacity planning.
- **High unhealthy-instance ratio** → health-check or dependency failure.
- **Sustained elevated latency/5xx** even after scaling → application bug, not capacity.
- **Manual override active** → human is driving the fleet.
- **Scaling policy violations / flapping** → misconfiguration.

**Manual override (safety valve):**
- Admin can **pause the autoscaler** or **set a fixed instance count**.
- Override **takes precedence** over automation, is **logged and alerted**, and has an **auto-expiry** so it cannot be forgotten.
- Re-enabling automation returns control to the control loop; changes are audited.

---

## Summary Flow

```
Metrics (CPU/latency/queue) → Threshold evaluation
   → Health check filter (healthy instances only)
   → Compute desired capacity (reactive + predictive + scheduled)
   → Scale up (fast) / scale down (slow)
   → Alert on anomalies / capacity limits
   └── Manual override can pause or fix count at any time
```

This gives fast spike absorption (queue/latency-driven), stable steady state (CPU target-tracking), self-healing (health checks + replacement), and a human escape hatch (override + alerts).

100%

task-0301-canary-rollout-of-a-service · Canary rollout of a service

Prompt 
You are rolling out v2 of an internal API to production and want to de-risk it. Describe the ordered plan for a staged canary release: deploy the new version to a small percentage of traffic, monitor error rates and latency, gradually increase the percentage as it stays healthy, and define the automatic rollback trigger and manual rollback procedure if metrics degrade.
Model answer 
# Staged Canary Release Plan for v2 API

## Phase 0 — Preparation (before any traffic)
1. **Baseline v1 metrics** — capture current error rate, p50/p95/p99 latency, saturation, and key business metrics over a stable window.
2. **Define SLOs and thresholds** — set explicit success/failure criteria (see Phase 4).
3. **Ensure routing capability** — configure load balancer / service mesh / feature flag to split traffic by version (e.g., header, weight, or user-segment routing).
4. **Ensure observability** — verify metrics, structured logging, and distributed tracing distinguish v1 vs v2.
5. **Smoke test v2** — deploy and validate with synthetic/zero real traffic.
6. **Confirm rollback path** — verify you can revert routing weights and redeploy the previous image/config instantly.

## Phase 1 — Initial canary (1–5% of traffic)
7. Deploy v2 alongside v1; route a small, representative slice (ideally a subset of users) to v2.
8. Hold at this level for a full observation window (e.g., 30–60 min, covering a business cycle).

## Phase 2 — Monitor
9. Track continuously: **error rate** (5xx, client errors, exceptions), **latency** (p50/p95/p99), **saturation** (CPU/memory), and **business metrics** (throughput, downstream dependency health).
10. Compare v2 against the v1 baseline in real time; watch for regressions, not just absolute values.

## Phase 3 — Gradual ramp (if healthy)
11. Increase in staged increments with hold periods at each: **5% → 25% → 50% → 75% → 100%**.
12. At each step, hold for the observation window and only proceed if metrics remain within SLOs.
13. Once v2 passes 100% and stabilizes, decommission v1.

## Phase 4 — Automatic rollback trigger
Roll back automatically (immediately, no human gate) if any condition holds for a sustained window (e.g., 5 min):
- **Error rate:** 5xx > 1% **or** > 2× the v1 baseline.
- **Latency:** p99 > absolute threshold (e.g., 500 ms) **or** > 20% above baseline.
- **Saturation:** CPU/memory consistently > 85–90%.
- **Any critical/paging alert** from tracing or dependency health.

Automation reverts traffic weights to v1 instantly (the safest, fastest revert).

## Phase 5 — Manual rollback procedure (if metrics degrade but don't auto-trigger, or for investigation)
1. **Halt the ramp** — pause further increases.
2. **Assess severity** — determine if it's a traffic-routing issue, a v2 bug, or a downstream/data problem.
3. **Shift traffic back** — move canary weight to 0% (route 100% to v1) to restore service.
4. **Investigate** — use traces/logs to root cause; capture evidence before discarding v2.
5. **Fix or revert deployment** — patch and re-canary, or roll back the v2 image/config entirely.
6. **Restore and verify** — confirm v1 is healthy, then decide to retry, defer, or abandon the release.
7. **Post-incident review** — document findings and adjust thresholds/process.

**Key principle:** automatic rollback = fast traffic reversion to v1; manual rollback = controlled halt, investigation, and either fix-and-retry or full revert.

task-0127-zigzag-conversion · Zigzag conversion

Prompt 
Write `convert(s, numRows)` that writes the string in a zigzag pattern across `numRows` rows and reads it row by row. Example: `convert("PAYPALISHIRING", 3)` returns `"PAHNAPLSIIGYIR"`.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `convert`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer 
export function convert(s, numRows) {
  if (numRows === 1) return s;
  const rows = Array.from({ length: numRows }, () => '');
  let cur = 0;
  let dir = -1;
  for (const c of s) {
    rows[cur] += c;
    if (cur === 0 || cur === numRows - 1) dir = -dir;
    cur += dir;
  }
  return rows.join('');
}

task-0272-secretary-task-ordering · Secretary task ordering

Prompt 
A scheduling secretary receives a list of `tasks`, where each task is [priority, labelIndex] but here each task is [priority, label]. Write `secretary(tasks)` returning the array of labels ordered by ascending priority; when two tasks share a priority, keep their original (input) order (a stable sort). The label is a string, priority is a non-negative integer.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function secretary(tasks) {
  return tasks
    .map((task, index) => ({ task, index }))
    .sort((a, b) => a.task[0] - b.task[0] || a.index - b.index)
    .map(({ task }) => task[1]);
}

task-0137-first-missing-positive · First missing positive

Prompt 
Write `firstMissingPositive(nums)` returning the smallest positive integer (>= 1) that is not present in the array. O(n) time, O(1) extra space.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `firstMissingPositive`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer 
export function firstMissingPositive(nums) {
  const n = nums.length;
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    while (nums[i] >= 1 && nums[i] <= n && nums[nums[i] - 1] !== nums[i]) {
      const j = nums[i] - 1;
      const tmp = nums[j];
      nums[j] = nums[i];
      nums[i] = tmp;
    }
  }
  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    if (nums[i] !== i + 1) {
      return i + 1;
    }
  }
  return n + 1;
}

task-0168-two-sum-fix-reuse · Two sum (fix reuse)

Return the indices (ascending) of the two numbers that add up to target. Exactly one solution exists and you may not use the same element twice.

```js
// BUGGY
export function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const m = new Map();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    m.set(nums[i], i);
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (m.has(need)) return [m.get(need), i].sort((a, b) => a - b);
```

Rewrite the function so it is correct. Output ONLY the corrected function as an ES module exporting `twoSum`. No markdown fences, no explanation.
Model answer 
export function twoSum(nums, target) {
  const m = new Map();
  for (let i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    const need = target - nums[i];
    if (m.has(need)) return [m.get(need), i].sort((a, b) => a - b);
    m.set(nums[i], i);
  }
}

task-0304-automate-an-on-call-runbook-response · Automate an on-call runbook response

Prompt 
Your team runs a manual on-call runbook: when a specific alert fires, the on-call engineer must ssh in, read logs, restart a service, and post to the status page. Automate this. Describe the ordered pieces you would build: define precise alert triggers, write an automation script that performs detection and remediation, wire it to alerting and a status page, add escalation if remediation fails, and cover testing the runbook safely.
Model answer 
## 1. Define precise alert triggers

The automation is only as good as its trigger. Vague alerts ("service slow") cause alert fatigue and unsafe auto-actions.

- **Single, specific signal per runbook.** Map each automated runbook to exactly one alert rule. If one alert needs multiple runbooks, split them.
- **Metric + threshold + duration.** Trigger on a concrete metric (e.g., `http_5xx_rate > 5%`, `process_state != running`, `p99_latency > 2s`) held *above* the threshold for a sustained window (e.g., 3–5 min) to reject transient blips.
- **Labels for routing.** Attach `service`, `instance`, `severity`, and `runbook_id` so the trigger knows which playbook to run and where.
- **Dedup + rate limiting.** Suppress duplicate pages and throttle repeat triggers (e.g., don't re-run the same remediation more than once per 15 min) to avoid restart storms.
- **Confidence gate.** Prefer composite signals (error rate *and* health-check failures) over a single metric to reduce false positives before any automated action.

## 2. Detection & remediation script

Build a small, idempotent **remediation engine** that runs a playbook of steps, rather than a monolithic script.

- **Detection/verification step first.** Before acting, re-confirm the incident (re-check the metric, confirm the process is actually down). This prevents acting on stale or false alerts.
- **Secure log access.** Read logs via the log aggregation API (Prometheus/Loki/CloudWatch) or a read-only service account, not raw SSH with shared credentials. If SSH is unavoidable, use short-lived, scoped keys from a secret manager.
- **Remediation step.** Restart via the correct control plane (`systemctl restart`, supervisor, orchestrator rollout) — not ad-hoc commands.
- **Idempotency.** Each step checks current state and no-ops if already in the desired state; the whole runbook is safe to re-run.
- **Verification step after.** Confirm recovery (health check passes, error rate drops) before declaring success.
- **Safety guards.** Max retries, per-step timeout, circuit breaker, and a max blast-radius limit. Every action is logged with who/what/when.

## 3. Wire to alerting and status page

- **Alerting → engine.** The monitoring system posts a webhook (with labels + context) to the remediation engine's event receiver. The engine claims the incident to prevent duplicate runs.
- **Engine → status page.** On incident start, post "investigating/maintenance" via the status page API; on recovery, post "resolved." Make posts **idempotent** (keyed by incident ID) so retries don't create duplicate entries.
- **Context in the post.** Include the trigger, what automation did, and whether it succeeded — so customers and engineers share one source of truth.
- **Feedback loop.** The status page and monitoring should reflect the same incident state.

## 4. Escalation if remediation fails

- **Retry with backoff.** Attempt remediation up to N times with increasing delay.
- **Time-based escalation.** If not resolved within a SLA window, escalate regardless of retries.
- **Human handoff with context.** Page the on-call engineer (Slack/phone) carrying: the alert, what was tried, relevant log excerpts, and a recommended action. Never page with a bare alert.
- **Human controls.** The engineer can approve, override, or abort the automation; the decision is logged for post-mortem.
- **Escalation ladder.** If the first responder doesn't acknowledge within X minutes, escalate to the next level / team lead.

## 5. Testing the runbook safely

Test without risking production — this is where automation goes wrong.

- **Unit/integration tests** for each step: detection logic, idempotency, and verification conditions.
- **Dry-run mode.** A flag that executes the full playbook in simulate-only mode (prints actions, touches nothing) — run this against real alerts before enabling auto-remediation.
- **Staging first.** Deploy and exercise the playbook end-to-end in a non-prod environment that mirrors production.
- **Controlled fault injection / game days.** In prod, trigger the fault deliberately (e.g., kill the process) during a change window with the runbook enabled, then verify auto-remediation + status page. Keep a rollback and a manual override ready.
- **Guardrails in every test:** timeouts, max blast radius, and the ability to flip to "monitor-only" instantly.
- **Post-test review.** Confirm no false status-page posts, no restart storms, and that escalation fired correctly.

---

**Cross-cutting:** least-privilege credentials via secret manager, full audit logging, and monitoring *the automation itself* (alert if the engine is down or a playbook is stale). Ship in stages: monitor-only → dry-run → auto-remediate with escalation, expanding coverage as confidence grows.
Agentic / Tool-use Level 2 PASS 9/9 cases
100%

task-0276-deadline-resource-scheduler · Deadline resource scheduler

Prompt 
A resource-scheduling agent must schedule the maximum number of tasks. Each task is [duration, deadline] (deadline exclusive; tasks are sequential and take integer time). The agent must pick a subset of tasks and an ordering so that each completes by its deadline. Write `schedule(tasks)` returning the maximum number of tasks that can all be scheduled. Algorithm: sort by deadline, process in order, and when the running total exceeds a deadline drop the longest-duration task so far.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function schedule(tasks) {
  const sorted = [...tasks].sort((a, b) => a[1] - b[1]);
  const chosen = [];
  let total = 0;
  for (const [dur, dl] of sorted) {
    chosen.push(dur);
    total += dur;
    if (total > dl) {
      let maxIdx = 0;
      for (let i = 1; i < chosen.length; i++) {
        if (chosen[i] > chosen[maxIdx]) maxIdx = i;
      }
      total -= chosen[maxIdx];
      chosen.splice(maxIdx, 1);
    }
  }
  return chosen.length;
}
Coding · Implementation Level 2 PASS 13/13 cases
100%

task-0097-string-to-integer-atoi · String to integer (atoi)

Prompt 
Write `myAtoi(s)` implementing the atoi algorithm: ignore leading whitespace; optional +/- sign; read digits; clamp to 32-bit signed range [-2^31, 2^31-1]. If no digits, return 0.

Output ONLY the ES module exporting `myAtoi`.
Model answer 
```javascript
export function myAtoi(s) {
  let i = 0;
  const n = s.length;

  // Skip leading whitespace
  while (i < n && s[i] === ' ') {
    i++;
  }

  // Determine optional sign
  let sign = 1;
  if (i < n && (s[i] === '+' || s[i] === '-')) {
    if (s[i] === '-') sign = -1;
    i++;
  }

  // Read digits, clamping on overflow
  const INT_MAX = 2 ** 31 - 1;
  const INT_MIN = -(2 ** 31);
  let result = 0;

  while (i < n && s[i] >= '0' && s[i] <= '9') {
    const digit = s.charCodeAt(i) - 48;
    if (result > (INT_MAX - digit) / 10) {
      return sign === 1 ? INT_MAX : INT_MIN;
    }
    result = result * 10 + digit;
    i++;
  }

  result *= sign;

  if (result > INT_MAX) return INT_MAX;
  if (result < INT_MIN) return INT_MIN;
  return result;
}
```

task-0303-downtime-free-database-schema-migration · Downtime-free database schema migration

Prompt 
You must add columns and restructure a core table in a large live database without downtime or data loss. Describe the ordered migration plan: create the new schema in parallel, backfill data into the new columns, switch the application to dual-write/read from both old and new, then cut over reads, and detail how you would verify and roll back.
Model answer 
# Zero-Downtime Schema Restructure: Ordered Migration Plan

**Core principle:** The old schema is the source of truth until the very end. Every phase is reversible via a feature flag, and the old table/columns are only dropped after the new schema has been proven correct under live traffic.

---

## Phase 1 — Create the new schema in parallel (no locks)

- Use an online schema-change path so the table is never locked: `gh-ost`/`pt-osc` for MySQL, `CREATE TABLE ... CONCURRENTLY` + concurrent index builds in PostgreSQL, or native `ALGORITHM=INPLACE` online DDL.
- If restructuring, create a **new table** (e.g., `users_v2`) with the target schema; if merely adding columns, add them **nullable** first.
- Build indexes concurrently to avoid blocking writes.
- Ensure the new schema is fully **backward compatible** — the currently deployed app version must keep working against the old schema unchanged.

## Phase 2 — Backfill historical data

- Migrate existing rows in **bounded batches** (e.g., 1k–10k rows/transaction) to keep each transaction short.
- Make the backfill **idempotent** and track a high-water mark so it can resume after failure.
- **Pace/throttle** the backfill against replication lag and CPU so it never starves live traffic.
- Capture concurrent changes during backfill (triggers or binlog/replication capture) so rows updated mid-migration aren't lost.
- Verify completeness before proceeding: row counts and checksums of the migrated set.

## Phase 3 — Dual-write and dual-read (shadow mode)

Deploy new app code behind a feature flag:

- **Dual-write:** write to the new schema *and* the old. Write to the **old first** (guaranteed), then the new with retry; if the new write fails, log and let reconciliation fix it. This guarantees the old schema never loses data.
- **Dual-read:** read from the new schema but periodically **compare against the old** and log discrepancies.
- Roll out as a **canary** (0.1% → 1% → 10% → 100% of traffic), watching error rates, latency (p99), and consistency metrics at each step.

## Phase 4 — Cut over

- **Reads:** once consistency holds over a sustained window, promote the new schema to primary for reads and route 100% of reads to it. Keep dual-write active as a safety net.
- **Writes:** switch writes so the new schema is the primary; then stop writing to the old schema.
- **Decommission:** after a soak period, drop the old table/columns and their indexes.

---

## Verification (continuous + final)

- **Structural:** row counts and per-key checksums comparing old vs. new (full scan, not just sampling).
- **Application-level:** business-logic assertions (e.g., recompute derived values, sanity-check distributions).
- **Operational:** error rate, latency percentiles, write throughput, replication lag, and DB CPU.
- **Negative tests:** inject bad input and confirm the new schema handles it as expected.
- Only proceed to the next phase when consistency and metrics are green.

## Rollback (safe because old schema stays live)

- **Fast revert:** flip the feature flag to point reads/writes back to the old schema. Because the old schema was never dropped and dual-write kept it in sync, this is near-instant and lossless.
- **Granular:** roll back the canary percentage first, then fully revert code.
- **Data-level rollback:** if only data drift is detected, re-run the idempotent backfill/reconciliation to resync the new schema rather than reverting the whole change.
- Keep point-in-time recovery / backups available throughout as the ultimate safety net.

---

**Why this order:** each phase de-risks the next — parallel creation avoids locks, backfill proves data can be transformed, dual-write/read proves correctness under real traffic before any traffic is moved, and keeping the old schema live until the end makes every step reversible.
Coding · Implementation Level 3 PARTIAL 5/7 cases

task-0135-sliding-window-maximum · Sliding window maximum

Prompt 
Write `maxSlidingWindow(nums, k)` returning an array containing the max of each contiguous subarray of length k, using a monotonic deque for O(n) time.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `maxSlidingWindow`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer 
```javascript
export function maxSlidingWindow(nums, k) {
  const n = nums.length;
  const result = [];
  if (n === 0 || k <= 0) return result;

  const deque = []; // stores indices, values in decreasing order
  let head = 0;

  for (let i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    // Drop the front index if it falls outside the window
    if (head < deque.length && deque[head] <= i - k) {
      head++;
    }
    // Maintain monotonic decreasing order: remove smaller values from the back
    while (head < deque.length && nums[i] >= nums[deque[deque.length - 1]]) {
      deque.pop();
    }
    // Reset head if the deque was emptied by popping
    if (head >= deque.length) {
      head = 0;
    }
    deque.push(i);
    // Record the max once the first full window is formed
    if (i >= k - 1) {
      result.push(nums[deque[head]]);
    }
  }

  return result;
}
```
Coding · Bugfix Level 2 PARTIAL 4/6 cases
67%

task-0215-count-negatives-in-grid · Count negatives in grid

Prompt 
Write `countNegatives(grid)` returning the number of negative numbers in an `m x n` grid where each row is sorted in decreasing order (and each column is sorted in decreasing order).

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function countNegatives(grid) {
  const m = grid.length;
  const n = grid[0].length;
  let row = 0;
  let col = n - 1;
  let count = 0;
  while (row < m && col >= 0) {
    if (grid[row][col] < 0) {
      count += col + 1;
      row++;
    } else {
      col--;
    }
  }
  return count;
}

task-0295-merge-latest-fix-into-feature-branch · Merge latest fix into feature branch

Prompt 
You are a coding agent. Your release branch `main` just received a critical bugfix. There is a long-running feature branch `feat/checkout` that must now include that fix, but should not be merged into `main` yet. Give the exact ordered sequence of git commands to bring the fix into the feature branch and switch back to that branch so work can continue on it.
Model answer 
## Approach: Cherry-pick the fix onto the feature branch

Since the goal is to bring **only the bugfix** from `main` into `feat/checkout` (not merge the whole branch), `cherry-pick` is the correct tool. A `git merge main` would pull in every commit on `main`, which is not desired here.

## Exact ordered sequence

```bash
# 1. Identify the bugfix commit hash on main
git log --oneline -n 5 main

# 2. Switch to the feature branch
git switch feat/checkout

# 3. Apply the bugfix commit onto the feature branch
git cherry-pick <bugfix-commit-hash>
```

## Notes

- Replace `<bugfix-commit-hash>` with the hash shown in step 1 (e.g., `a1b2c3d`).
- If the fix spans multiple commits, list them all after `cherry-pick`: `git cherry-pick <hash1> <hash2> ...`.
- After step 3 you are already on `feat/checkout`, ready to continue work — `main` is untouched.
- If `cherry-pick` produces conflicts, resolve them, then `git cherry-pick --continue`; abort with `git cherry-pick --abort` if needed.

task-0077-merge-sorted-arrays-in-place · Merge sorted arrays in place

Prompt 
You are given two sorted integer arrays nums1 and nums2. nums1 has length m+n but only the first m elements are meaningful (rest are 0). Write `merge(nums1, m, nums2, n)` that merges them in place and returns nums1, sorted ascending.

Output ONLY the ES module exporting `merge`.
Model answer 
export function merge(nums1, m, nums2, n) {
  let p1 = m - 1;
  let p2 = n - 1;
  let p = m + n - 1;

  while (p1 >= 0 && p2 >= 0) {
    if (nums1[p1] > nums2[p2]) {
      nums1[p] = nums1[p1];
      p1--;
    } else {
      nums1[p] = nums2[p2];
      p2--;
    }
    p--;
  }

  while (p2 >= 0) {
    nums1[p] = nums2[p2];
    p2--;
    p--;
  }

  return nums1;
}

task-0258-rotate-array-right-by-k · Rotate array right by k

Prompt 
Write `rotate(nums, k)` in Python returning the array rotated to the RIGHT by `k` steps (elements move right, wrapping). `k` can exceed array length. Do it in O(1) extra space (reverse trick) and return the array.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
def rotate(nums, k):
    n = len(nums)
    if n == 0:
        return nums
    k %= n

    def reverse(i, j):
        while i < j:
            nums[i], nums[j] = nums[j], nums[i]
            i += 1
            j -= 1

    reverse(0, n - 1)
    reverse(0, k - 1)
    reverse(k, n - 1)
    return nums
Coding · Algorithm Level 2 PARTIAL 3/8 cases
38%

task-0265-largest-number-from-array · Largest number from array

Prompt 
Write `largestNumber(nums)` returning the string formed by arranging the numbers so they concatenate to the largest possible number. Return it as a string (strip leading zeros).

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
```js
export function largestNumber(nums) {
  const sorted = nums.map(String).sort((a, b) => (b + a) < (a + b) ? 1 : (b + a) > (a + b) ? -1 : 0);
  const result = sorted.join('');
  return result[0] === '0' ? '0' : result;
}
```

task-0292-word-ladder-agent · Word ladder agent

Prompt 
A translation agent transforms one word into another by changing a single letter at a time, where each intermediate word must be in `wordList`. Write `wordLadder(beginWord, endWord, wordList)` returning the length of the shortest transformation chain counting the beginWord as step 1 (so directly changing to endWord from beginWord is length 2). Return 0 if no such chain exists. All words have the same length and are lowercase. If beginWord equals endWord return 1.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function wordLadder(beginWord, endWord, wordList) {
    if (beginWord === endWord) return 1;

    const wordSet = new Set(wordList);
    if (!wordSet.has(endWord)) return 0;

    let level = 1;
    let current = [beginWord];
    const visited = new Set([beginWord]);

    while (current.length > 0) {
        const next = [];
        for (const word of current) {
            const chars = word.split('');
            for (let i = 0; i < chars.length; i++) {
                const original = chars[i];
                for (let c = 'a'; c <= 'z'; c++) {
                    if (c === original) continue;
                    chars[i] = c;
                    const candidate = chars.join('');
                    if (candidate === endWord) {
                        return level + 1;
                    }
                    if (wordSet.has(candidate) && !visited.has(candidate)) {
                        visited.add(candidate);
                        next.push(candidate);
                    }
                }
                chars[i] = original;
            }
        }
        current = next;
        level++;
    }

    return 0;
}

task-0148-number-of-islands · Number of islands

Prompt 
Write `numIslands(grid)` where grid is a 2D array of "0" (water) and "1" (land). Count the number of islands, where an island is a group of '1's connected 4-directionally (up/down/left/right).

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting `numIslands`. No markdown fences, no explanation, no tests.
Model answer 
export function numIslands(grid) {
  if (!grid || grid.length === 0 || grid[0].length === 0) return 0;

  const rows = grid.length;
  const cols = grid[0].length;
  let count = 0;

  const visited = Array.from({ length: rows }, () => new Array(cols).fill(false));

  const dfs = (r, c) => {
    if (r < 0 || r >= rows || c < 0 || c >= cols) return;
    if (visited[r][c] || grid[r][c] !== "1") return;

    visited[r][c] = true;
    dfs(r + 1, c);
    dfs(r - 1, c);
    dfs(r, c + 1);
    dfs(r, c - 1);
  };

  for (let r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
    for (let c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
      if (grid[r][c] === "1" && !visited[r][c]) {
        count++;
        dfs(r, c);
      }
    }
  }

  return count;
}

task-0230-number-of-islands · Number of Islands

Prompt 
Write `num_islands(grid)` in Python. `grid` is an m x n list of '0'/'1' strings where '1' is land. Return the number of islands, where an island is 1-cells connected horizontally or vertically.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
def num_islands(grid):
    if not grid or not grid[0]:
        return 0
    
    m, n = len(grid), len(grid[0])
    visited = [[False] * n for _ in range(m)]
    
    def dfs(i, j):
        if i < 0 or i >= m or j < 0 or j >= n:
            return
        if grid[i][j] != '1' or visited[i][j]:
            return
        visited[i][j] = True
        dfs(i + 1, j)
        dfs(i - 1, j)
        dfs(i, j + 1)
        dfs(i, j - 1)
    
    count = 0
    for i in range(m):
        for j in range(n):
            if grid[i][j] == '1' and not visited[i][j]:
                dfs(i, j)
                count += 1
    return count

task-0260-next-permutation · Next permutation

Prompt 
Write `nextPermutation(nums)` returning the lexicographically next greater permutation of `nums` (in place). If no next greater permutation exists, return the array in ascending order. Return the resulting array.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function ...`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer 
export function nextPermutation(nums) {
  const n = nums.length;
  let i = n - 2;
  while (i >= 0 && nums[i] >= nums[i + 1]) {
    i--;
  }
  if (i >= 0) {
    let j = n - 1;
    while (nums[j] <= nums[i]) {
      j--;
    }
    [nums[i], nums[j]] = [nums[j], nums[i]];
  }
  let left = i + 1;
  let right = n - 1;
  while (left < right) {
    [nums[left], nums[right]] = [nums[right], nums[left]];
    left++;
    right--;
  }
  return nums;
}

task-0227-count-triplets-equal-sum · Count triplets equal sum

Prompt 
Write `triplet_count(nums, target)` in Python returning the number of index triplets (i, j, k) with i < j < k such that `nums[i] + nums[j] + nums[k] === target`.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
```python
def triplet_count(nums, target):
    nums.sort()
    n = len(nums)
    count = 0
    for i in range(n - 2):
        left = i + 1
        right = n - 1
        t = target - nums[i]
        while left < right:
            s = nums[left] + nums[right]
            if s < t:
                left += 1
            elif s > t:
                right -= 1
            else:
                if nums[left] == nums[right]:
                    c = right - left + 1
                    count += c * (c - 1) // 2
                    break
                left_count = 1
                right_count = 1
                while left + 1 < right and nums[left] == nums[left + 1]:
                    left += 1
                    left_count += 1
                while right - 1 > left and nums[right] == nums[right - 1]:
                    right -= 1
                    right_count += 1
                count += left_count * right_count
                left += 1
                right -= 1
    return count
```

task-0095-string-compression · String compression

Prompt 
Write `compress(chars)` that compresses the array of characters in place using run-length encoding, so consecutive characters are replaced with the character followed by its count (only if count > 1). Return the new length of the (prefix of the) array.

Output ONLY the ES module exporting `compress`.
Model answer 
export function compress(chars) {
  let write = 0;
  let i = 0;
  while (i < chars.length) {
    const char = chars[i];
    let count = 0;
    while (i < chars.length && chars[i] === char) {
      count++;
      i++;
    }
    chars[write++] = char;
    if (count > 1) {
      for (const c of String(count)) {
        chars[write++] = c;
      }
    }
  }
  return write;
}

task-0165-prime-check · Prime check

Prompt 
Return True if n is a prime number (n >= 2).

```python
# BUGGY
import math
def is_prime(n):
    for i in range(2, n // 2):
        if n % i == 0:
            return False
    return True
```

Rewrite the function so it is correct. Output ONLY the corrected `def is_prime(n):`. No markdown fences, no explanation.
Model answer 
```python
def is_prime(n):
    if n < 2:
        return False
    for i in range(2, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            return False
    return True
```

task-0006-tcp-slow-start-window-growth · TCP Slow Start Window Growth

Prompt 
During the slow start phase of TCP congestion control, how does the congestion window grow?
Model answer 
**Exponentially** — the congestion window doubles each round-trip time (RTT).

During slow start, TCP increments cwnd by 1 MSS for each ACK received. This causes the window to grow exponentially (1 → 2 → 4 → 8 → ... MSS), allowing rapid ramp-up of the transmission rate until it reaches a threshold (ssthresh) or packet loss occurs.

  const a = m + n - 2;

task-0042-two-liar-knight-compound · Two liar-knight compound

Prompt 
A and B are from a land where knights always tell the truth and knaves always lie. A says: "B and I are both knights." B says: "A is a knave." Who is who?
Model answer 
**A is a knave, B is a knight.**

**Reasoning:**

Suppose A is a knight. Then A's statement ("B and I are both knights") is true, so B is also a knight. But then B's statement ("A is a knave") would be true — meaning A is a knave. **Contradiction.**

So A must be a **knave**. Then A's statement is false (consistent, since they aren't both knights). B says "A is a knave," which is **true** — so B is a **knight**.

✓ A (knave) lies; B (knight) tells the truth.

The standard solution:

```
function palindromePairs(words) {
    const map = new Map();
    for (let i = 0; i < words.length; i++) {
        map.set(words[i], i);
    }
    
    const result = [];
    for (let i = 0; i < words.length; i++) {
        const word = words[i];
        for (let k = 0; k <= word.length; k++) {
            const left = word.slice(0, k);
            const right = word.slice(k);
            
            if (isPalindrome(left)) {
                const rightRev = reverse(right);
                if (map.has(rightRev)) {
                    const j = map.get(rightRev);
                    if (i !== j) {
                        // rightRev + word = palindrome
                        result.push([j, i]);
                    }
                }
            }
            
            if (isPalindrome(right)) {
                const leftRev = reverse(left);
                if (map.has(leftRev)) {
                    const j = map.get(leftRev);
                    if (i !== j) {
                        // word + leftRev = palindrome
                        result.push([i, j]);
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return result;
}
```

```python
    def palindromePairs(self, words: List[str]) -> List[List[int]]:
        lookup = {word: i for i, word in enumerate(words)}
        res = []
        for i in range(len(words)):
            for j in range(len(words[i]) + 1):
                left = words[i][:j]
                right = words[i][j:]
                if left == left[::-1]:

                if right == right[::-1]:

                    if rev_left in lookup and lookup[rev_left] != i:
                        res.append([i, lookup[rev_left]])
        return res
```

task-0037-causality-vs-correlation · Causality vs correlation

Prompt 
Studies find that ice cream sales and drowning deaths both increase during summer. Which conclusion is most defensible?
Model answer 
**C. The correlation is likely due to a third variable (hotter summer weather), not a direct causal link between the two.**

Ice cream sales and drownings are correlated, but neither causes the other. Instead, a confounding variable—warm summer weather—drives both: more people buy ice cream in the heat, and more people swim, increasing drowning risk. This is a classic example of **correlation ≠ causation**.

task-0044-paradox-self-reference · Paradox self-reference

Prompt 
Consider the statement: "This statement is false." Which best describes the situation?
Model answer 
This is the classic **liar paradox** — a self-referential paradox. The statement cannot be consistently assigned a truth value: if it is true, then it must be false (since it claims to be false), and if it is false, then it must be true (since it correctly describes itself as false). This creates an unresolvable logical contradiction.

task-0022-floating-point-accumulation · Floating-point accumulation

Prompt 
```js
let sum = 0;
for (let i = 0; i < 10; i++) sum += 0.1;
console.log(sum === 1.0);
```
What does this print?
Model answer 
**false**

Adding `0.1` ten times in floating point yields `0.9999999999999999` (not exactly `1.0`), because `0.1` cannot be represented exactly in binary floating point. So the strict equality check `sum === 1.0` evaluates to `false`.

A vending machine agent is given the `price` of an item and a list of `coins` inserted by a customer (coin values in cents). If the total inserted is less than the price, return null. Otherwise return the change owed as an array [quarters, dimes, nickels, pennies] using coin values 25, 10, 5, 1 cents and as few coins as possible (greedy). Return null when the customer paid less than the price.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer (error) 
export function makeChange(price, coins) {
  const total = coins.reduce((sum, c) => sum + c, 0);
  if (total < price) return null;
  const change = total - price;
  const quarters = Math.floor(change / 25);
  let rem = change % 25;
  const dimes = Math.floor(rem / 10);
  rem %= 10;
  const nickels = Math.floor(rem / 5);
  rem %= 5;
  const pennies = rem;
  return [quarters, dimes, nickels, pennies];
}

task-0263-count-reverse-pairs · Count reverse pairs

Prompt 
Write `reverse_pairs(nums)` in Python returning the number of pairs (i, j) with i < j and `nums[i] > 2 * nums[j]`. Use a merge-sort-based count, O(n log n).

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def ...`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer 
```python
def reverse_pairs(nums):
    def merge_sort(arr):
        if len(arr) <= 1:
            return arr
        mid = len(arr) // 2
        left = merge_sort(arr[:mid])
        right = merge_sort(arr[mid:])
        return merge(left, right)

    def merge(left, right):
        count = 0
        j = 0
        for i in range(len(left)):
            while j < len(right) and left[i] > 2 * right[j]:
                j += 1
            count += j
        merged = []
        p = q = 0
        while p < len(left) and q < len(right):
            if left[p] <= right[q]:
                merged.append(left[p])
                p += 1
            else:
                merged.append(right[q])
                q += 1
        merged.extend(left[p:])
        merged.extend(right[q:])
        return merged

    return merge_sort(nums)
```
Reasoning Level 3 FAIL
0%

task-0043-weighing-ambiguous-weight · Weighing ambiguous weight

Prompt 
You have 12 balls, one odd (heavier OR lighter, unknown which) and a balance scale giving three outcomes. You have exactly 3 weighings. In the first weighing you place balls 1-4 left and 5-8 right, and they balance. Which statement correctly describes the situation?
Model answer 
**B** — The odd ball must be one of 9, 10, 11, or 12, and with 2 weighings remaining (plus 8 known-good reference balls) it can be identified and its heavy/light status determined.

**Reasoning:** Since 1–4 balanced against 5–8, all eight of those balls are normal. The odd ball is therefore among 9–12. This leaves 4 candidates × 2 (heavy or light) = 8 possibilities, and 2 remaining weighings yield 3² = 9 outcomes, so it is solvable. For example: weigh 9, 10, 11 against three good balls — a balance isolates ball 12 (one more weighing settles heavy/light), while a tilt narrows it to the three and reveals whether the odd ball is heavy or light.

task-0007-direct-mapped-cache-set-selection-bits · Direct-Mapped Cache Set Selection Bits

Prompt 
In a direct-mapped cache with 2^n sets, which bits of the memory address are used to determine the cache set?
Model answer 
**The n bits immediately above the offset bits (bits n through 2n−1).**

In a direct-mapped cache, the address is split into three fields:

- **Tag** — the most significant bits, used to verify which block is cached
- **Set index** — the **n bits** just above the offset, used to select one of the 2ⁿ sets
- **Offset** — the least significant bits (log₂ of block size), used to select the word within the block

Since there are 2ⁿ sets, exactly **n bits** are needed to index the set, and these are the n bits located directly above the offset field.
Coding · Implementation Level 2 FAIL 0/5 cases

```python
def topKFrequent(nums, k):
```

task-0002-tcp-vs-udp-delivery-guarantee · TCP vs UDP Delivery Guarantee

Prompt 
Which feature is provided by TCP but NOT by UDP?
Model answer 
**Reliable delivery (connection-oriented service)**

TCP provides reliability features that UDP lacks, including:
- **Connection-oriented** (handshake before data transfer)
- **Acknowledgments & retransmission** (guaranteed delivery)
- **Ordered delivery** (sequence numbers)
- **Flow control** and **congestion control**
- **Error recovery**

UDP is connectionless and unreliable—it sends datagrams with no guarantee of delivery, ordering, or protection against loss.

task-0293-cheapest-flight-within-k-stops · Cheapest flight within k stops

Prompt 
A booking agent finds the cheapest flight itinerary. There are `n` cities (0..n-1). `flights` is a list of [from, to, price] directed flights. Travel from `src` to `dst` using at most `k` stops (intermediate cities, so at most k+1 flights). Write `findCheapestPrice(n, flights, src, dst, k)` returning the cheapest price, or -1 if no itinerary with at most k stops exists. Use Bellman-Ford style relaxation over k+1 rounds.

Output ONLY the function definition as an ES module exporting the function (e.g. `export function f(...)`). Do not include markdown fences, explanation, wrapper script, or tests.
Model answer (error) 
export function findCheapestPrice(n, flights, src, dst, k) {
  const dist = new Array(n).fill(Infinity);
  dist[src] = 0;

  for (let round = 0; round <= k; round++) {
    const temp = dist.slice();
    for (const [from, to, price] of flights) {
      if (dist[from] === Infinity) continue;
      const candidate = dist[from] + price;
      if (candidate < temp[to]) {
        temp[to] = candidate;
      }
    }
    dist = temp;
  }

  return dist[dst] === Infinity ? -1 : dist[dst];
}

```python
def longest_consecutive(nums):
    s = set(nums)
    best = 0
    for n in s:
        if n - 1 not in s:
            cur = n
            length = 1
            while cur in s:
                cur += 1
                length += 1
            best = max(best, length)
    return best
```

task-0290-paragraph-formatter · Paragraph formatter

Prompt 
A printing agent formats a paragraph with full justification. Given `words` (a list of strings, no spaces) and `max_width`, return a list of lines each exactly `max_width` characters long. Greedily pack as many words as fit; a single-word (or last) line is left-justified (left-aligned, padded with trailing spaces), while other lines evenly distribute extra spaces between words, with any remaining extra spaces going to the leftmost gaps. Every line must have exactly `max_width` characters.

Output ONLY the function definition (e.g. `def f(...)`). No markdown fences, no explanation, no main block.
Model answer (error) 
```python
def full_justification(words, max_width):
    result = []
    i = 0
    n = len(words)
    while i < n:
        line = [words[i]]
        i += 1
        while i < n and len(' '.join(line)) + len(words[i]) <= max_width:
            line.append(words[i])
            i += 1
        if i >= n or len(line) == 1:
            text = ' '.join(line)
            result.append(text + ' ' * (max_width - len(text)))
        else:
            total_words_len = sum(len(w) for w in line)
            extra_spaces = max_width - total_words_len
            num_gaps = len(line) - 1
            base = extra_spaces // num_gaps
            remainder = extra_spaces % num_gaps
            text = ''
            for j in range(num_gaps):
                text += line[j]
                text += ' ' * base
                if j < remainder:
                    text += ' '
            text += line[-1]
            result.append(text)
    return result
```

```js
    fns.push(function() { return i; });
  }
  return fns;
}
const f = makeAll();
console.log(f[0]());
```
What does this print?
Model answer 
3
Code Comprehension Level 2 FAIL
0%

task-0018-loop-closure-over-let · Loop closure over let

Prompt 
```js
const fns = [];
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) {
  fns.push(() => i);
}
console.log(fns[0](), fns[2]());
```
What does this print?
Model answer 
`0 2`

With `let`, each loop iteration creates a fresh binding of `i`, so each closure captures its own value: `fns[0]()` returns `0` and `fns[2]()` returns `2`. (This differs from `var`, which would print `2 2`.)
Code Comprehension Level 2 FAIL
0%

task-0031-truth-teller-or-liar · Truth-teller or liar

Prompt 
On an island there are two tribes: Knights, who always tell the truth, and Knaves, who always lie. You meet a person who says: "I am a knave." What does this statement imply?
Model answer 
**The statement is self-contradictory (a paradox) — it cannot be made by anyone.**

Here's why:

- **If the person is a Knight** (always truthful): Saying "I am a knave" would be a lie, which a Knight cannot do. ❌ Contradiction.

- **If the person is a Knave** (always lying): Saying "I am a knave" would be the truth, which a Knave cannot do. ❌ Contradiction.

Since neither a Knight nor a Knave can utter "I am a knave," the statement is logically impossible.
